```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Training-free\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance.} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, and is attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \citep{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\% with one relation to 50.4\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\% to 38.7\% with 96 relations (document-level), near-halving or worse. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, which places them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.
5	
6	What explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \citep{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
7	
8	This study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.
9	
10	We ask three questions. \textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \textbf{RQ2}: Through what modes do count and confusability each degrade performance? \textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?
11	
12	To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We quantify confusability using pairwise embedding similarity, the \emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), and its batch-level aggregation, the \emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \citep{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \citep{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \citep{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \citep{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\S\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).
13	
14	Our experiments yield four findings:
15	(1)~Degradation operates along two dimensions with bounded interaction: count-related recall decline (up to $-$49\,pp) and confusability-related type substitution, confirmed on sentence- and document-level RE (\S\ref{sec:count_curves}, \S\ref{sec:multi_channel}).
16	(2)~Pairwise SCS predicts type confusion across NER, RE, and ED (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}); at batch level, BCS predicts RE accuracy independent of output format (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) but not NER accuracy; per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
17	(3)~Effects are consistent across schema sizes, prompt designs, and models from three architecture families (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).
18	(4)~Schema decomposition fails due to merge amplification (NER, ED) and routing failure (RE), despite oracle routing recovering substantial accuracy (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}).
19	Several findings are non-obvious: modest pairwise signals ($r^2 {\approx} 1.3\%$) amplify to $R^2 {=}\, 22\text{--}58\%$ at batch level; renaming confusable types \emph{decreases} accuracy (Appendix~\ref{app:renaming}); and few-shot demonstrations widen rather than close the confusability gap.
20	
21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]
23	    \item We present what is, to our knowledge, the first controlled study that isolates count and confusability effects in training-free LLM extraction. Building on the embedding-similarity criterion from prior schema-partitioning work \citep{llm-pee-2025}, we design stratified experiments that quantify confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation across NER, RE, and event detection (\S\ref{sec:count_curves}, \S\ref{sec:pairwise}, \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).
24	    \item We document a task asymmetry: BCS explains 22--58\% of RE accuracy variance through type substitution---an effect confirmed as format-independent---yet shows no batch-level signal in NER despite significant pairwise effects (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}, \S\ref{sec:multi_channel}).
25	    \item We identify structural barriers to confusability-aware extraction: schema decomposition fails due to merge amplification (NER, ED) and routing failure (RE), establishing that the decompose-then-merge paradigm faces fundamental precision--recall tradeoffs in training-free IE (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}).
26	\end{enumerate}
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}
5	The two-dimensional decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). Approximate separability is confirmed on FewRel and DocRED (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}); logit-scale evidence remains inconclusive. Both dimensions replicate across four models from three architecture families (8B--70B open-weight; Gemini 2.5 Flash via API), two decoding formats, five prompt designs, and two RE datasets.
6	
7	\paragraph{Embedding similarity and format independence.}
8	SCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space, amplifying from pairwise $R^2 = 1.3\%$ to batch-level 22--58\% via correlated aggregation (Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}). The BCS--performance relationship holds across all RE settings but not NER; a paired format comparison confirms format independence (Appendix~\ref{app:format-independence}). The task asymmetry reflects error-mode structure: RE errors concentrate in type substitutions \citep{tam-2024-let-me-speak}, whereas NER errors scatter across miss, boundary, and type modes \citep{schall-2025-hidden-cost}. SCS likely proxies deeper conceptual overlap persisting despite label renaming (Appendix~\ref{app:renaming}); we hypothesize high-SCS types intensify softmax competition---testable via logit-space analysis.
9	
10	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
11	For NER, reducing candidate types per prompt is the primary lever; for RE, we suggest flagging type pairs with SCS${}\,>\,0.85$ and monitoring batch-level BCS (typical gap 2--4\,pp in random batches; extreme-BCS contrasts reach 25--31\,pp).
12	
13	\paragraph{Conclusion.}
14	\label{sec:conclusion}
15	Training-free LLM extraction degrades along two separable dimensions---count-associated recall decline and confusability-associated type substitution---replicated on four models from three architecture families (8B--70B open-weight; Gemini 2.5 Flash via API); decomposition fails via merge amplification and routing failure.
16	
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
3	
4	We evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).
5	
6	\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}
7	\label{sec:datasets}
8	
9	\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}
10	Few-NERD \citep{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains and is suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 instances uniformly from the test set; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.
11	
12	\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}
13	FewRel \citep{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). A held-out validation confirms SCS--confusion generalization across the split boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:scs-heldout-validation}). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per relation type) eliminates type frequency as a potential confound; this balance makes it the primary dataset for BCS analysis.
14	
15	\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}
16	DocRED \citep{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.
17	
18	\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}
19	MAVEN \citep{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology; all types belong to a single ontology (FrameNet), so this is a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification. MAVEN serves a supplementary role because its same-domain schema produces centrality confounds at the batch level (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \S\ref{sec:scs}.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{\# Types} & \textbf{\# Eval Units} & \textbf{Domain} & \textbf{BCS Range} & \textbf{Embedding} \\
30	\midrule
31	Few-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\
32	FewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\
33	DocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\
34	MAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\rlap{$^\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range: min--max over count-curve batches. Embedding: model used for SCS/BCS; ranges are not comparable across embedding models. $^\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences.}
38	\label{tab:datasets}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}
42	\label{sec:scs}
43	
44	We define the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\text{embed}(\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:
45	{\small
46	\[
47	\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \cos\bigl(\text{embed}(\text{name}(t_i)),\; \text{embed}(\text{name}(t_j))\bigr)
48	\]
49	}
50	
51	For a batch of types $B = \{t_1, \ldots, t_k\}$, the \textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:
52	\[
53	\text{BCS}(B) = \frac{1}{\binom{k}{2}} \sum_{i < j,\; t_i, t_j \in B} \text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)
54	\]
55	
56	We use two embeddings to provide complementary perspectives: Qwen3 captures the extraction model's own type representations (self-similarity), while MiniLM provides an independent general-purpose baseline. Cross-embedding consistency holds on all datasets (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}); MiniLM consistently yields equal or stronger correlations, which confirms that Qwen3-based estimates are conservative. We compute SCS with: (1)~Qwen3-32B \citep{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d) and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \citep{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-d sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 because its fine-grained same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\ ``person-actor'') require the higher-dimensional space. MiniLM is the primary BCS embedding because sentence-transformers are trained for semantic similarity \citep{sentence-bert-2019}; Qwen3 causal-LM states show compressed BCS range (std${}\,=\,0.034$ vs.\ MiniLM std${}\,=\,0.081$), consistent with autoregressive anisotropy \citep{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}. Name-only SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy to name+definition ($\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\ref{app:scs-ablation}).
57	
58	\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}
59	\label{sec:scs-validation}
60	SCS is a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction. Pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}), though context, frequency, and definition quality also contribute (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}).
61	
62	\begin{figure*}[t]
63	\centering
64	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
65	\centering
66	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}
67	\end{minipage}
68	\hfill
69	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}
72	\end{minipage}
73	\caption{\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves. Error bars: $\pm 1$\,SD across 3 seeds $\times$ 25 batches. RE declines monotonically; NER shows a U-shape. Rightmost points are full-schema (negligible sampling variance). \textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Slopes are consistent across the independent and within-pool 16-relation conditions ($p = 0.60$); wider BCS range at 80 relations produces larger gaps.}
74	\label{fig:main_results}
75	\end{figure*}
76	
77	\subsection{Experimental Design}
78	\label{sec:exp_design}
79	
80	We conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.
81	
82	\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}
83	We vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 25 random type batches for Few-NERD and FewRel (5 for DocRED and MAVEN)\footnote{DocRED and MAVEN use fewer batches due to higher per-batch evaluation cost (document-level RE and 8{,}042 sentences, respectively).} and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The remaining count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \{2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 66\} for Few-NERD, \{4, 8, 16, 32, 40, 64, 80\} for FewRel, \{4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\} for DocRED, and \{6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for MAVEN.
84	
85	\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}
86	We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). Extreme batches are constructed via greedy search over the pairwise SCS matrix: the low-BCS batch starts from the least-similar type pair and iteratively adds the type with lowest maximum similarity to already-selected types; the high-BCS batch mirrors this with maximum similarity. We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\,\in\{6, 12\}$), FewRel (count$\,\in\{8, 16\}$, both 16-relation (pilot) and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\,\in\{16, 32\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
87	
88	\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}
89	\label{sec:metrics}
90	
91	\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). Each batch evaluates only samples whose gold relation is included in the candidate set; out-of-schema samples are excluded before inference. The prompt allows a \texttt{none} response for cases where the model judges no candidate applies; any such prediction is scored as incorrect since the gold is always among the candidates by construction. \textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.
92	
93	
94	\subsection{Model and Inference}
95	\label{sec:model}
96	
97	We use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \citep{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \citep{vllm-2023} on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96\,GB) with greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints that double precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \citep{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) and Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which substantially reduces model-level randomness (residual SD = 0.88\,pp at count 80 under greedy decoding, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism); bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.
98

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nTraining-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows, yet prior work conflates two factors: type count and type confusability. We present what is, to our knowledge, the first controlled study isolating these factors across NER, RE, and event detection on four datasets. We quantify confusability via the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and its batch-level aggregation, the Batch Confusability Score (BCS). Degradation operates along two dimensions with task-dependent effect structures: count-related recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp on DocRED 96-relation RE) and confusability-related type substitution. In RE, BCS explains 22--58\\% of cross-batch accuracy variance (FewRel/DocRED), independent of output format (paired constrained/unconstrained comparison yields virtually identical correlations); NER shows no batch-level BCS signal, as errors scatter across missed entities and boundary failures. Schema decomposition fails due to merge amplification (NER, ED) and routing failure (RE). {

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance.} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, and is attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\citep{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 50.4\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level), near-halving or worse. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, which places them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\citep{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what modes do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We quantify confusability using pairwise embedding similarity, the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\citep{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\citep{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\citep{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\citep{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield four findings:\n(1)~Degradation operates along two dimensions with bounded interaction: count-related recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp) and confusability-related type substitution, confirmed on sentence- and document-level RE (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}, \\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n(2)~Pairwise SCS predicts type confusion across NER, RE, and ED (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}); at batch level, BCS predicts RE accuracy independent of output format (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) but not NER accuracy; per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n(3)~Effects are consistent across schema sizes, prompt designs, and models from three architecture families (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n(4)~Schema decomposition fails due to merge amplification (NER, ED) and routing failure (RE), despite oracle routing recovering substantial accuracy (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\nSeveral findings are non-obvious: modest pairwise signals ($r^2 {\\approx} 1.3\\%$) amplify to $R^2 {=}\\, 22\\text{--}58\\%$ at batch level; renaming confusable types \\emph{decreases} accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{app:renaming}); and few-shot demonstrations widen rather than close the confusability gap.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item We present what is, to our knowledge, the first controlled study that isolates count and confusability effects in training-free LLM extraction. Building on the embedding-similarity criterion from prior schema-partitioning work \\citep{llm-pee-2025}, we design stratified experiments that quantify confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation across NER, RE, and event detection (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}, \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n    \\item We document a task asymmetry: BCS explains 22--58\\% of RE accuracy variance through type substitution---an effect confirmed as format-independent---yet shows no batch-level signal in NER despite significant pairwise effects (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}, \\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n    \\item We identify structural barriers to confusability-aware extraction: schema decomposition fails due to merge amplification (NER, ED) and routing failure (RE), establishing that the decompose-then-merge paradigm faces fundamental precision--recall tradeoffs in training-free IE (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}",

      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 14,
        "newLines": 7,

          " (2)~Pairwise SCS predicts type confusion across NER, RE, and ED (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}); at batch level, BCS predicts RE accuracy independent of output format (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) but not NER accuracy; per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).",
          "-(3)~Effects are consistent across schema sizes, prompt designs, and models from three architecture families (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).",

SCS does not capture context-dependent disambiguation or mention-level ambiguity; these factors contribute to the residual variance in pairwise analysis.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\citep{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains and is suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 instances uniformly from the test set; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\citep{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). A held-out validation confirms SCS--confusion generalization across the split boundary (Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-heldout-validation}). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per relation type) eliminates type frequency as a potential confound; this balance makes it the primary dataset for BCS analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\citep{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\citep{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology; all types belong to a single ontology (FrameNet), so this is a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification. MAVEN serves a supplementary role because its same-domain schema produces centrality confounds at the batch level (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range: min--max over count-curve batches. Embedding: model used for SCS/BCS; ranges are not comparable across embedding models. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe define the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe use two embeddings to provide complementary perspectives: Qwen3 captures the extraction model's own type representations (self-similarity), while MiniLM provides an independent general-purpose baseline. Cross-embedding consistency holds on all datasets (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}); MiniLM consistently yields equal or stronger correlations, which confirms that Qwen3-based estimates are conservative. We compute SCS with: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\citep{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d) and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\citep{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-d sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 because its fine-grained same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') require the higher-dimensional space. MiniLM is the primary BCS embedding because sentence-transformers are trained for semantic similarity \\citep{sentence-bert-2019}; Qwen3 causal-LM states show compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with autoregressive anisotropy \\citep{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}. Name-only SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy to name+definition ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS is a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction. Pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), though context, frequency, and definition quality also contribute (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves. Error bars: $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches. RE declines monotonically; NER shows a U-shape. Rightmost points are full-schema (negligible sampling variance). \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Slopes are consistent across the independent and within-pool 16-relation conditions ($p = 0.60$); wider BCS range at 80 relations produces larger gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 25 random type batches for Few-NERD and FewRel (5 for DocRED and MAVEN)\\footnote{DocRED and MAVEN use fewer batches due to higher per-batch evaluation cost (document-level RE and 8{,}042 sentences, respectively).} and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The remaining count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 66\\} for Few-NERD, \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). Extreme batches are constructed via greedy search over the pairwise SCS matrix: the low-BCS batch starts from the least-similar type pair and iteratively adds the type with lowest maximum similarity to already-selected types; the high-BCS batch mirrors this with maximum similarity. We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation (pilot) and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). Each batch evaluates only samples whose gold relation is included in the candidate set; out-of-schema samples are excluded before inference. The prompt allows a \\texttt{none} response for cases where the model judges no candidate applies; any such prediction is scored as incorrect since the gold is always among the candidates by construction. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\citep{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\citep{vllm-2023} on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96\\,GB) with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints that double precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\citep{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) and Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which substantially reduces model-level randomness (residual SD = 0.88\\,pp at count 80 under greedy decoding, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism); bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\citep{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains and is suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 instances uniformly from the test set; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\citep{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). A held-out validation confirms SCS--confusion generalization across the split boundary (Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-heldout-validation}). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per relation type) eliminates type frequency as a potential confound; this balance makes it the primary dataset for BCS analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\citep{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\citep{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology; all types belong to a single ontology (FrameNet), so this is a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification. MAVEN serves a supplementary role because its same-domain schema produces centrality confounds at the batch level (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range: min--max over count-curve batches. Embedding: model used for SCS/BCS; ranges are not comparable across embedding models. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe define the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe use two embeddings to provide complementary perspectives: Qwen3 captures the extraction model's own type representations (self-similarity), while MiniLM provides an independent general-purpose baseline. Cross-embedding consistency holds on all datasets (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}); MiniLM consistently yields equal or stronger correlations, which confirms that Qwen3-based estimates are conservative. We compute SCS with: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\citep{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d) and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\citep{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-d sentence encoder. Few-NERD uses Qwen3 because its fine-grained same-category pairs (e.g., ``person-artist'' vs.\\ ``person-actor'') require the higher-dimensional space. MiniLM is the primary BCS embedding because sentence-transformers are trained for semantic similarity \\citep{sentence-bert-2019}; Qwen3 causal-LM states show compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with autoregressive anisotropy \\citep{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}. Name-only SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy to name+definition ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS is a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction. Pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion (Few-NERD $r = 0.112$, FewRel $r = 0.187$, both $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), though context, frequency, and definition quality also contribute (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). SCS does not capture context-dependent disambiguation or mention-level ambiguity; these factors contribute to the residual variance in pairwise analysis.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves. Error bars: $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches. RE declines monotonically; NER shows a U-shape. Rightmost points are full-schema (negligible sampling variance). \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Slopes are consistent across the independent and within-pool 16-relation conditions ($p = 0.60$); wider BCS range at 80 relations produces larger gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 25 random type batches for Few-NERD and FewRel (5 for DocRED and MAVEN)\\footnote{DocRED and MAVEN use fewer batches due to higher per-batch evaluation cost (document-level RE and 8{,}042 sentences, respectively).} and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The remaining count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 66\\} for Few-NERD, \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). Extreme batches are constructed via greedy search over the pairwise SCS matrix: the low-BCS batch starts from the least-similar type pair and iteratively adds the type with lowest maximum similarity to already-selected types; the high-BCS batch mirrors this with maximum similarity. We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation (pilot) and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1, where a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy (exact match of predicted relation label to gold). Each batch evaluates only samples whose gold relation is included in the candidate set; out-of-schema samples are excluded before inference. The prompt allows a \\texttt{none} response for cases where the model judges no candidate applies; any such prediction is scored as incorrect since the gold is always among the candidates by construction. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1, where any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\citep{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\citep{vllm-2023} on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96\\,GB) with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints that double precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\citep{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) and Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation with greedy decoding, which substantially reduces model-level randomness (residual SD = 0.88\\,pp at count 80 under greedy decoding, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism); bootstrap CIs capture the remaining sampling variance from batch composition. Pair-level CIs use percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe two-dimensional decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). Approximate separability is confirmed on FewRel and DocRED (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}); logit-scale evidence remains inconclusive. Both dimensions replicate across four models from three architecture families (8B--70B open-weight; Gemini 2.5 Flash via API), two decoding formats, five prompt designs, and two RE datasets.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding similarity and format independence.}\nSCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space, amplifying from pairwise $R^2 = 1.3\\%$ to batch-level 22--58\\% via correlated aggregation (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}). The BCS--performance relationship holds across all RE settings but not NER; a paired format comparison confirms format independence (Appendix~\\ref{app:format-independence}). The task asymmetry reflects error-mode structure: RE errors concentrate in type substitutions \\citep{tam-2024-let-me-speak}, whereas NER errors scatter across miss, boundary, and type modes \\citep{schall-2025-hidden-cost}. Approximate separability is confirmed on FewRel and DocRED (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}); logit-scale evidence remains inconclusive. {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "beada21c-1d37-49f1-8c00-6b343a69946f",
  "uuid": "650c1094-e006-4221-a886-5e2f094c0825",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/results.tex",
    "oldString": "inconclusive on the logit scale ($p = 0.209$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).",
    "newString": "inconclusive on the logit scale ($p = 0.209$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}): limited power ($n = 69$ batches) produces wide CIs, and the logit-scale point estimate back-transforms to 2.53--2.78\\,pp across observed baselines, still within the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}). The pattern is stable across three seeds (max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD; intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$). Count curves establish trend direction; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$) and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. Confusion rate is bidirectional $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by gold frequency sum; CIs are percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) excluded (per-instance RE format). Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs exclude zero (Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]).}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\citep{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. While pairwise $r^2$ is modest, correlated type pairs within batches compound this signal: batch-level BCS explains 22--58\\% of accuracy variance (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). The independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), which indicates that shared-representation bias is conservative. SCS outperforms simpler baselines (Levenshtein, WordNet Wu-Palmer, same-parent; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-baselines}). Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis} lists the most confusable type pairs with concrete examples.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition (Few-NERD NER and FewRel RE). IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS across all evaluated datasets ($\\rho = 0.84\\text{--}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} includes DocRED). RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nBCS explains 22--58\\% of cross-batch RE accuracy variance; confusability is a substantial predictor alongside count. We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate this effect. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison on 100 random batches; based on a single extreme batch per BCS level at 16-rel scale; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (95\\% CI [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16) and confirms monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema ($n{=}55$ per count level): extreme-BCS gap $-$25.0\\,pp at count 16 ($\\rho = -0.762$) and $-$15.7\\,pp at count 32 ($\\rho = -0.693$; both $p < 0.001$). Pooled across counts, BCS explains 58\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.578$, $n = 110$; 95\\% CI [0.46, 0.68]; per-count: $R^2 = 0.653$ at count 16, $0.614$ at count 32), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$).\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nDirect regression on 100 random 16-type batches from the 80-relation pool shows BCS explains 22\\% of cross-batch accuracy variance ($R^2 = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$), scaling to 58\\% on DocRED (pooled, $n = 110$). The typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI [0.4, 4.0]). Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). A post-hoc Monte Carlo simulation confirms the ${\\sim}3\\times$ pair-to-batch amplification (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: extreme-BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Gemini 2.5 Flash replicates Dimension~2 at batch level: low-BCS 90.5\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 72.9\\% ($\\Delta = -17.6$\\,pp, 95\\% CI [15.1, 20.1], $n = 50$) at count 8, with the smaller gap likely reflecting ceiling compression from Gemini's higher baseline. Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) confirms directional consistency (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_70b}, \\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}). LLaMA-3.1-8B replicates the BCS effect on FewRel RE ($\\Delta{=}{-}27.1$\\,pp) but shows no batch-level signal on NER, consistent with the task asymmetry observed across all model sizes.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp ($r = -0.919$, $p < 0.001$; $n{=}25$), modestly attenuated from v1 but highly significant. NER shows no batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under either prompt ($r \\approx 0$, $n = 450$; \\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}). The effect holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r \\leq -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), ruling out prompt design as the source. Few-shot demonstrations widen the confusability gap (+4.3\\,pp under 5-shot), though this finding is limited to a single dataset and count level (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS across all three datasets: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for Few-NERD NER, $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for FewRel sentence-level RE, and $\\rho = 0.843$ ($p < 10^{-30}$, $n = 110$) for DocRED document-level RE. The slightly lower DocRED correlation reflects additional noise from document-level context and cross-sentence reasoning. In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic; confusable types impair entity detection itself. In DocRED, IntraSub accounts for 72\\% of errors in high-BCS batches vs.\\ 15\\% in low-BCS batches; ExtraSub is zero (forced-choice format). Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion). The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. We select the equivalence margin via convergent evidence: (i)~an interaction exceeding the primary BCS effect (IQR-predicted $\\Delta = 2.2$\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) would represent ${\\geq}100\\%$ amplification, fundamentally altering whether the dimensions are independently addressable; (ii)~Cohen's $d = 0.5$ (medium effect) at the within-cell SD of 4.6\\,pp yields $\\pm 2.3$\\,pp. Both converge on $\\pm 2\\text{--}3$\\,pp. TOST \\citep{lakens-2017-equivalence,schuirmann-1987-tost} on FewRel bounds the interaction at $\\pm 3$\\,pp ($p = 0.003$); at the stricter $\\pm 2$\\,pp margin, equivalence is marginal ($p = 0.047$) and inconclusive on the logit scale ($p = 0.209$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). On DocRED ($n{=}55$ per count level), the raw factorial interaction is $-$9.4\\,pp (95\\% CI [$-$12.9, $-$6.0]), but this is confounded by narrower BCS ranges at count 32 (spread 0.26 vs.\\ 0.38). Restricting both count levels to the overlapping BCS range [0.43, 0.70] reduces the interaction from 7.52\\,pp (CI [1.49, 12.83]) to 0.93\\,pp (CI [$-$7.13, 7.44])---consistent with bounded interaction, though the reduced sample ($n = 29{+}55$) has 80\\% power only for effects ${\\geq}10.5$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). In NER, batch-level BCS shows no predictive signal (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), where NER errors distribute across miss, boundary, and type modes rather than concentrating in type substitution (relaxed matching confirms this null is not a metric artifact; Appendix~\\ref{app:relaxed-matching}); the factorial test is inconclusive ($p = 0.50$).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation ($\\rho = +0.184\\text{--}0.208$, $p < 0.03$): FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly \\citep{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}, conflating confusability with centrality. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}), though the pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$). Few-NERD's cross-domain schema shows the expected negative direction ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$); mean BCS is reliable only for cross-domain schemas.",

NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}). The pattern is stable across three seeds (max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD; intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$). Count curves establish trend direction; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$) and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. Confusion rate is bidirectional $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by gold frequency sum; CIs are percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) excluded (per-instance RE format). Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\citep{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. While pairwise $r^2$ is modest, correlated type pairs within batches compound this signal: batch-level BCS explains 22--58\\% of accuracy variance (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). The independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), which indicates that shared-representation bias is conservative. SCS outperforms simpler baselines (Levenshtein, WordNet Wu-Palmer, same-parent; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-baselines}). Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis} lists the most confusable type pairs with concrete examples.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition (Few-NERD NER and FewRel RE). FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison on 100 random batches; based on a single extreme batch per BCS level at 16-rel scale; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}).           " Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS across all three datasets: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for Few-NERD NER, $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for FewRel sentence-level RE, and $\\rho = 0.843$ ($p < 10^{-30}$, $n = 110$) for DocRED document-level RE. The slightly lower DocRED correlation reflects additional noise from document-level context and cross-sentence reasoning. In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic; confusable types impair entity detection itself. In DocRED, IntraSub accounts for 72\\% of errors in high-BCS batches vs.\\ 15\\% in low-BCS batches; ExtraSub is zero (forced-choice format). Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.",

          "-These results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion). We select the equivalence margin via convergent evidence: (i)~an interaction exceeding the primary BCS effect (IQR-predicted $\\Delta = 2.2$\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) would represent ${\\geq}100\\%$ amplification, fundamentally altering whether the dimensions are independently addressable; (ii)~Cohen's $d = 0.5$ (medium effect) at the within-cell SD of 4.6\\,pp yields $\\pm 2.3$\\,pp. Both converge on $\\pm 2\\text{--}3$\\,pp. TOST \\citep{lakens-2017-equivalence,schuirmann-1987-tost} on FewRel bounds the interaction at $\\pm 3$\\,pp ($p = 0.003$); at the stricter $\\pm 2$\\,pp margin, equivalence is marginal ($p = 0.047$) and inconclusive on the logit scale ($p = 0.209$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}): limited power ($n = 69$ batches) produces wide CIs, and the logit-scale point estimate back-transforms to 2.53--2.78\\,pp across observed baselines, still within the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin. On DocRED ($n{=}55$ per count level), the raw factorial interaction is $-$9.4\\,pp (95\\% CI [$-$12.9, $-$6.0]), but this is confounded by narrower BCS ranges at count 32 (spread 0.26 vs.\\ 0.38).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}). The pattern is stable across three seeds (max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD; intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$). Count curves establish trend direction; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$) and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. Confusion rate is bidirectional $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by gold frequency sum; CIs are percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) excluded (per-instance RE format). Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs exclude zero (Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]).}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\citep{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. While pairwise $r^2$ is modest, correlated type pairs within batches compound this signal: batch-level BCS explains 22--58\\% of accuracy variance (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). The independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), which indicates that shared-representation bias is conservative. SCS outperforms simpler baselines (Levenshtein, WordNet Wu-Palmer, same-parent; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-baselines}). Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis} lists the most confusable type pairs with concrete examples.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition (Few-NERD NER and FewRel RE). IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS across all evaluated datasets ($\\rho = 0.84\\text{--}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} includes DocRED). RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nBCS explains 22--58\\% of cross-batch RE accuracy variance; confusability is a substantial predictor alongside count. We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate this effect. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison on 100 random batches; based on a single extreme batch per BCS level at 16-rel scale; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (95\\% CI [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16) and confirms monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema ($n{=}55$ per count level): extreme-BCS gap $-$25.0\\,pp at count 16 ($\\rho = -0.762$) and $-$15.7\\,pp at count 32 ($\\rho = -0.693$; both $p < 0.001$). Pooled across counts, BCS explains 58\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.578$, $n = 110$; 95\\% CI [0.46, 0.68]; per-count: $R^2 = 0.653$ at count 16, $0.614$ at count 32), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$).\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nDirect regression on 100 random 16-type batches from the 80-relation pool shows BCS explains 22\\% of cross-batch accuracy variance ($R^2 = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$), scaling to 58\\% on DocRED (pooled, $n = 110$). Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). A post-hoc Monte Carlo simulation confirms the ${\\sim}3\\times$ pair-to-batch amplification (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: extreme-BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Gemini 2.5 Flash replicates Dimension~2 at batch level: low-BCS 90.5\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 72.9\\% ($\\Delta = -17.6$\\,pp, 95\\% CI [15.1, 20.1], $n = 50$) at count 8, with the smaller gap likely reflecting ceiling compression from Gemini's higher baseline. Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) confirms directional consistency (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_70b}, \\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}). LLaMA-3.1-8B replicates the BCS effect on FewRel RE ($\\Delta{=}{-}27.1$\\,pp) but shows no batch-level signal on NER, consistent with the task asymmetry observed across all model sizes.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp ($r = -0.919$, $p < 0.001$; $n{=}25$), modestly attenuated from v1 but highly significant. NER shows no batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under either prompt ($r \\approx 0$, $n = 450$; \\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}). The effect holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r \\leq -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), ruling out prompt design as the source. Few-shot demonstrations widen the confusability gap (+4.3\\,pp under 5-shot), though this finding is limited to a single dataset and count level (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS across all three datasets: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for Few-NERD NER, $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for FewRel sentence-level RE, and $\\rho = 0.843$ ($p < 10^{-30}$, $n = 110$) for DocRED document-level RE. The slightly lower DocRED correlation reflects additional noise from document-level context and cross-sentence reasoning. In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic; confusable types impair entity detection itself. In DocRED, IntraSub accounts for 72\\% of errors in high-BCS batches vs.\\ 15\\% in low-BCS batches; ExtraSub is zero (forced-choice format). Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion). The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. We select the equivalence margin via convergent evidence: (i)~an interaction exceeding the primary BCS effect (IQR-predicted $\\Delta = 2.2$\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) would represent ${\\geq}100\\%$ amplification, fundamentally altering whether the dimensions are independently addressable; (ii)~Cohen's $d = 0.5$ (medium effect) at the within-cell SD of 4.6\\,pp yields $\\pm 2.3$\\,pp. Both converge on $\\pm 2\\text{--}3$\\,pp. Margin sensitivity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) confirms equivalence is robust: the regression TOST is significant at $\\pm 2$\\,pp ($p = 0.047$) and strengthens monotonically through $\\pm 5$\\,pp. TOST \\citep{lakens-2017-equivalence,schuirmann-1987-tost} on FewRel bounds the interaction at $\\pm 3$\\,pp ($p = 0.003$); at the stricter $\\pm 2$\\,pp margin, equivalence is marginal ($p = 0.047$) and inconclusive on the logit scale ($p = 0.209$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}): limited power ($n = 69$ batches) produces wide CIs, and the logit-scale point estimate back-transforms to 2.53--2.78\\,pp across observed baselines, still within the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin.
